Crystal Palace: Six managers who’d keep Eagles up as Sage sack decision beckons

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Pierre Sage’s time as Crystal Palace manager could be up after a 3-1 loss to Liverpool sank them to the bottom of the Premier League table.

Sunderland beating Chelsea means Palace have been overtaken in the relegation zone and now sit in last spot. Sage’s side have only won one of their last eight games in all competitions and it’s now four losses on the bounce in the league.

The international break is looming and it could be the perfect time for Palace to make a change in the dugout. But who could they attract?

Here are some reasonably realistic options that could get Palace out of their rut and potentially keep them up.

One of the most appealing names out there in terms of available managers with a respectable body of work in the Premier League.

The last time Howe was an available manager, after leaving Bournemouth in 2020, he took his time to get the right next job. But when Newcastle came calling in 2021, it turned out to be a match made in heaven.

There were highs and lows in Howe’s time at St. James’ Park as he became the face of a new era under new owners. But he was like a breath of fresh air for the Newcastle faithful who’d grown weary of what was being served up by Steve Bruce.

Howe eventually led Newcastle to their first trophy in decades by winning the EFL Cup in 2025. His reign also included a pair of Champions League qualifications.

His time with Newcastle proved he knows how to take a club struggling at the wrong end of the table and lead them to new heights.

Sure, he wouldn’t have the same kind of financial backing at Palace as he did at Newcastle, but Howe didn’t need it at Bournemouth and worked wonders there.

The only question is whether he’d see Palace as an opportunity or a step below him – especially if there’s a vacancy at Manchester United…

Again, this might be an ambitious one, but there’s not too much by the way of fresh opportunities for Slot right now after his sacking by Liverpool earlier this year.

It doesn’t seem to be bothering him too much. He recently went on a trip to South America to take in some games at a handful of iconic venues. He’s also believed to have said no to Fulham, AC Milan, Al-Ahli and the Netherlands since leaving Liverpool.

But his reason for rejecting his national team’s job was because of an itch to be coaching day in, day out. Now he’s had a few months out of the game, could he be more tempted now if a club like Palace gave him that chance again?

Palace actually had a look at him in 2023, reportedly making contact with the then-Feyenoord boss when they were looking for a long-term replacement for Patrick Vieira.

Slot, already an Eredivisie winner with Feyenoord by that point, boosted his reputation by winning the Premier League with Liverpool at the first time of asking. His stock dropped with a feeble title defence, but Palace are the kind of club he could prove himself with again.

If dreaming of Howe or Slot might be a bit above Palace’s station right now, then it might be time to press the Dyche button.

You know what you’re getting with Dyche and his tactics. Organisation. Discipline. Back to the basics.

Palace have tried to go for more adventurous styles in the past but have sometimes found it better when they’ve gone down the kind of route that Dyche would symbolise.

It may not have worked out for him at Nottingham Forest last season, but the situation there was a mess. It wasn’t all his fault.

At 55, Dyche still has something to give in management if he fancies it. It might not be the most attractive option, but of the realistic ones, it could be one of the most sensible.

If Howe or Slot would be too ambitious and Dyche wouldn’t be ambitious enough, Frank might be the happy medium.

His work with Brentford was excellent, helping to establish them as the stable Premier League club they are today. There were times during his reign when he’ll have had to look over his shoulders – and he steered the ship in the right direction.

Tottenham fans will have a different view of Frank’s ability, but if he wants to prove that was just a blip, Palace could be the kind of club to help him get back on track.

Give him time and Frank should be able to deliver some better results, all while building a stable project.

Pereira’s success at keeping Forest up last season should not be overlooked. He was an important factor in them ending the season strongly enough to avoid the danger of the drop.

There’d be a degree of irony if he was to take the Palace job after being replaced by Oliver Glasner, who of course was Sage’s direct predecessor at Selhurst Park.

Ask Palace fans and they’d surely rather have Glasner back after the cup-winning success he enjoyed with them, but that doesn’t look possible right now.

Pereira isn’t a manager to be dismissed from a shortlist so easily. Is he capable of building a long-term project? Maybe not. But he gave Forest a spark when they needed it last season and that’s what Palace could do with now.

Look, it’s (probably) not going to happen. The man’s retired. Numerous times.

But when Palace have been in bother before, Hodgson has consistently come to their rescue.

He hasn’t managed since his encore of a spell with Bristol City earlier this year and is unlikely to do so again.

For that and various other reasons, it’d be seen as a step backwards to revert to him in a caretaker role again, but if you’re wanting a manager who can keep Palace up, he’s been there and done that.

Manchester United fans made their feelings unmistakably clear at Old Trafford on Sunday, erupting into a loud cheer when full-back Diogo Dalot was substituted off during a chaotic afternoon against Aston Villa.

The reaction from the Old Trafford crowd was as brutal. When the board went up in the 66th minute indicating Dalot’s number was being replaced by Patrick Dorgu, a noticeable wave of cheers echoed through the stands.

The loud ovation was not one of appreciation, but rather sarcasm filled frustration after another poor defensive display.

Dalot’s tough afternoon began early as United found themselves trailing 2-0 within four minutes following goals from Alysson and Nicolas Jackson.

After United fought back to tie the game at 2-2 through Bruno Fernandes and Youri Tielemans before the break, Dalot’s second half dissolved into disaster.

In the 47th minute, the Portuguese defender committed a clumsy foul on Alysson inside the penalty area, allowing John McGinn to convert from the spot.

Six minutes later, Leon Goretzka bundled home a fourth for Villa, leaving Dalot completely exposed and compelling Michael Carrick to make the change.

The substitution proved to be game changer. Trailing 4-2, United mounted a heroic late rally, coming back from a two-goal deficit for the second time in the match.

Dalot’s replacement Dorgu was at the heart of the comeback, with a goal and assist.

Here are the best electric bikes you can buy at every price level in November 2026

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

I’ve spent countless hours here at Electrek doing detailed hands-on testing of hundreds of electric bikes. Through thousands of miles of riding, I’ve been fortunate to learn these e-bikes inside and out, top to bottom and front to back. That long-term experience with real-world e-bike testing has helped me find the best electric bicycles on the market for just about any budget.

Below are some of the top e-bikes I’ve hand-tested for every price range, current as of November 2026. These are probably the best deals you’ll find all year on electric bikes, meaning this is the best time ever to get a new model! Check out the awesome e-bikes below, any one of which could become your next electric bike.

This list is divided into several price ranges. We’ll start with the best e-bikes under $1,000, then move on to the under-$2,000 category, under-$3,000 category, etc. The goal is to highlight some of my favorite models in various budget ranges to help you make the right decision based on your needs and budget.

The sub-$1,000 electric bike category is critical for a few reasons.

First, it’s pretty hard to produce a quality e-bike and sell it for under $1k. Most of the options in this price category make too many sacrifices in quality or longevity to earn a spot on this list as one of “the best.”

Second, many newly curious e-bike buyers just aren’t prepared to drop several thousand dollars on a brand-new concept, making this price range critical for many new e-bike shoppers.

Fortunately, we’ve found some excellent options for cheap electric bikes that can still save you some serious dough.

The Lectric XP LITE 3.0 was recently updated and is now quite likely the king of bang-for-your-buck electric bikes.

For a while now, the $999 Lectric XP 4 has reigned supreme in the budget electric bike category. That’s still a great bike, and we’ll discuss it next, but the more affordable $799 Lectric XP LITE3 is an even more wallet-friendly electric bike.

That new e-bike offers some great riding at under 800 bucks.

It’s a throttle-enabled Class 2 folding electric bike with 20″ wheels, a max speed of 20 mph (32 km/h), and a motor rated for 300W continuous and 819W peak. It’s not going to be the strongest hill climber in this list, but it does get going quickly on flat and modestly hilly terrain.

And the biggest features include quality hydraulic disc brakes, color LCD screen, turn signals, and even an optional Gates carbon belt drive system for an extra $100. Riders can also opt for a nearly double-sized battery for an extra $200.

This is the only e-bike in this price range that I know of with a 48V electrical system. All the other budget e-bikes under $800 use a 36V system and thus suffer from weaker power.

There are some things that you give up with this model, such as that the fenders aren’t included free. But for just $799, you’re still getting built-in lights, a hidden battery, large color LCD display and a lightweight folder. Plus it comes in lots of fun color options.

The Lectric XP4 is the undisputed best-selling electric bike in the US. The bike has racked up massive sales figures as riders flock to its extreme bang-for-your-buck. Simply put, it’s hard to find any other e-bike that offers this level of value!

The XP4 can get riders up to 28 mph (45 km/h) and now comes in either a 500W or 750W version, with respective battery sizes of 48V 10.4Ah and 48V 17.5Ah. The long-range version’s premium price tag ($1,299 vs the standard range model at $999) is perhaps even higher value than the already impressive base model.

Other upgrades include a new stronger rack design, torque sensor for smoother pedal assist, and improved hardware like the 8-speed transmission, hydraulic disc brakes, updated urban/trail hybrid-style tire tread, and a beautiful new TFT color screen.

The XP4 is hands-down the highest value e-bike I’ve ever tested and it’s quite obvious just how much time and effort went into building such a potent e-bike designed to fit so many different riders and tasks.

It’s hard to find a better combination of low cost and high performance anywhere in the e-bike market right now.

When Ride1Up launched the original Portola, it was pretty obvious what the game plan was: fire a shot across Lectric Ebike’s bow with a direct competitor to the Lectric XP line. By all accounts, Ride1Up nailed it with an impressive competitor. Now the company has come back with the next generation of the bike, this time attempting to take on both the XP line and XP Lite from Lectric. That’s thanks to two models, the Portola 02, and the stripped-down Portola 02 Lite.

The Ride1Up Portola 02 has a lot more going for it than just the attractive starting price of $895 for the Lite or $995 for the full-size bike with frame-integrated rack. The models offer great performance with a maximum speed of 28 mph (45 km/h) and good quality components including hydraulic disc brakes, torque sensor, front suspension, a built-in rear rack (except for the Lite), and a 750W motor.

There are two battery options available, either a 48V 10Ah or a 48V 15Ah, and the larger battery only costs an extra $100, so it’s quite worth the extra 50% range.

I love the sleek-looking frame and apparently, I’m not alone. Those who are on the hunt for a good-looking folding fat tire e-bike that doesn’t break the bank will likely find this to be one of the more fashionable options.

Once you bump up above the $1,000 limit, you start to see many more great options for e-bikes in a wide range of categories.

From electric cargo bikes to cruisers and even electric mopeds, there’s something for everyone in this price range.

The $1,249 JackRabbit OG2 is in one of these fuzzy areas between e-bikes and other e-rideables. In this case, it straddles the line between an electric bike and an electric scooter.

It more or less feels like an electric bike based on its ride geometry. However, the lack of pedals means that it could also be classified as an electric scooter, albeit a large-wheeled e-scooter.

But with those 20-inch wheels and bicycle seat, the JackRabbit OG2 rides much more like a small folding e-bike than an e-scooter. And its electronics match that of a light e-bike, too.

The 300W motor gets the little runabout up to 20 mph (32 km/h). The small 158 Wh battery only offers around 10 miles (approximately 16 km) of range, but is also conveniently small enough to easily keep a spare battery in a cargo pocket or even a small purse.

The biggest benefit of the JackRabbit is that it is so simple that the lack of frills keeps the weight down to just 25 lb (11.3 kg)! For an e-bike, that’s almost so light that it floats.

The company also released a higher power and larger battery capacity version too, which might be good for riders who want to push harder and go farther. For those that want to see a few more upgrades on this minimalist e-bike, the JackRabbit OG2 and XG might be more your size.

See my full review of the JackRabbit OG2 here.

We called the Lectric XPedition e-cargo bike a runaway success right from the start, and that’s exactly what it turned into. Now with the new Lectric XPedition 2.0 on the road, it’s better than ever.

With heavy-hauling weight capacity, the option for one or two batteries for extra long range, hydraulic disc brakes and torque sensor-based intuitive feeling pedal assist, this e-bike is dragging electric cargo bikes into the future.

The $1,399 Lectric XPedition 2.0 has turned the electric cargo bike market upside down, bringing a heavy-hitting cargo e-bike to market for an ultra-affordable price.

While it’s not the fanciest cargo e-bike on the market (you’ll find those in the $4,000-$6,000 price range further down this article), it’s definitely the best bang for your buck thanks to a combination of high value parts and reasonable pricing.

The Heybike Ranger 3 Pro is probably one of the best deals in full-suspension folding e-bikes right now. With a combination of a powerful motor, comfortable geometry, front & rear suspension, and a high-tech interface that includes NFC card unlocking, the Heybike Ranger 3 Pro is punching above its weight class.

In fact, that’s its main drawback, its weight. It’s a heavy bike, but the speed and power mean heavier motors and batteries, so you know you’re going to be carrying a more robust powertrain around with you.

But between the performance and the looks, this is one awesome comfortable ride that just doesn’t break the bank! I had a blast reviewing it, and I’ll bet you’ll have a blast riding it!

The $1,399 Aventon Soltera.2 is the latest version of Aventon’s beautiful city bike that offers some of the best quality fabrication that I’ve seen in this price range – or even in higher price ranges. It is offered in either a single-speed or seven-speed format.

The bike rolls on narrow tires for that efficient city ride, while still packing in a 500W motor.

The battery is a bit smaller at 36V and 10Ah, but the bike makes up for a smaller battery by being able to integrate it into the frame in a sleek and attractive package.

The beautiful color display also adds to the bike’s charm, as do the powerful headlight and frame-integrated tail light. There’s even an awesome companion app that lets your phone track your bike’s important stats. Basically, you’re getting a lot for $1,199 here!

Check out my in-depth Aventon Soltera.2 review here.

Ride1Up has proven itself as a high-value electric bike brand, focusing largely on efficient and speedy commuter e-bikes. While the company has many models in the $1,000 to $2,000 range, the $1,295 Ride1Up Roadster V3 is one of the best bang-for-your-buck options in the lightweight category.

This 500W-rated e-bike offers Class 3 performance, meaning you can hit speeds of up to 28 mph (45 km/h), or a still impressive 20 mph (32 km/h) if using the throttle. But sticking to pedal-assist means you get to enjoy that quality torque sensor for smoother pedaling. The bike even comes with an option for either a belt drive or a 10-speed Shimano transmission, and three frame sizes to choose from.

The V3’s design was updated with many new features, including a now-removable battery for even more convenient charging both on and off the bike. And with a sub-40 lb weight, it’s hard to beat this one on a pound-for-pound basis!

Check out my full review of the Ride1Up Roadster V3 here:

Lectric Ebikes expanded into larger territory with the launch of the Lectric XPeak2. It was the company’s first e-bike with a larger than 20″ wheel size, bringing some chunky 26×4.0″ tires into the company’s inventory.

The XPeak takes the form of a typical adventure-style fat tire electric bike. It features a hardtail design with a suspension fork, high-volume tires, hydraulic disc brakes, and a relatively large battery that is certified by the Swiss testing agency SGS to meet UL 2849 standards.

The bike is also tested to higher standards for ruggedness of the frame and fork than most e-bikes in this price range, meaning it’s likely going to last longer too. As far as fat tire adventure-style e-bikes go, there’s no better bang for your buck than the Lectric XPeak2.

The $2,295 Ride1Up Prodigy V2 Eco has brought new meaning to the phrase “low-cost mid-drive electric bike”, updating an already impressive e-bike with a new next-gen setup.

This e-bike sports a high-end German-manufactured Brose mid-drive motor and can hit speeds of up to 28 mph (45 km/h), yet is price similarly to much cheaper hub motor-based electric bikes.

It’s a perfect commuter e-bike for someone who wants high-quality parts like that German mid-drive motor with 90Nm of torque, hydraulic disc brakes, bright LED lights, included fenders, rear rack, and a very comfortable saddle. Plus, there’s a choice between a step-over or a mid-step frame to better fit a wider variety of riders.

This e-bike is easily worth another $700 more than the price tag, if not more. In fact, you’ll find many of these same components on e-bikes with $2,000-$3,000 price tags all over the place. So if you’re looking for a higher quality e-bike that doesn’t cost as much as a fancy bike shop e-bike, this is the one to consider.

The Ride1Up Vorsa is Ride1Up’s most versatile “SUV‑style” e‑bike yet, designed to transition seamlessly from commuting through city streets to loaded errands and light gravel or park paths. With a standard price around US $1,495, it delivers an exceptionally high‑value riding experience that often outperforms models far above its price range.

With a powerful 750 W rear‑hub motor producing a hefty 95 Nm of torque (and peaking over 1,400 W in real-world tests), the Vorsa accelerates comfortably and can maintain Class 3 speeds of up to 28 mph via pedal assist or 20 mph throttle thanks to its dual-sensor system and customizable display. Whether you’re hauling cargo or cruising commute lanes, the Vorsa delivers smooth, responsive power that feels natural and planted.

I love how well‑rounded the package is. Ride1Up outfits the Vorsa with a 100 mm suspension fork, comfortable ergonomic bars, multiple colors/frame styles (step‑thru or standard), and a modular rear rack rated for 440 lb total payload, which is expandable via Connect+ accessories. Hydraulic disc brakes, full fenders, integrated lighting, and passive features like UL‑certified batteries and Apple FindMy integration round out the build.

At just $1,695, the Ride1Up Vorsa is priced way below fair market value, which makes this an absolute steal of a deal.

Check out my Ride1Up Vorsa review here, and also check out the new expansion to the Vorsa line with lighter and heavier duty models built on the Vorsa platform.

The Marker is the first model unveiled by the e-bike brand spun out of Lectric eBikes, known as Monarc. The brand leverages Lectric’s size and buying power to bring higher end bikes than we typically see in the budget space – complete with higher shelf components – but at better prices and with better customer service.

The Monarc Marker is a powerful, Class 3-capable fat tire adventure e-bike with powerful four-piston brakes, a quality reverse arch suspension fork, Shimano CUES transmission, and comes with two batteries, all for an incredible reasonable MSRP of US $1,999.

Monarc also made waves by offering a 5-year warranty as well as 12-hour customer service five days a week, ensuring customers feel comfortable knowing they are buying from a company that will stick around to support riders long after the purchase is complete.

Learn more about the Monarc Marker here, or check out its current sale price here.

When Juiced returned with a vengeance after being saved by Lectric, the brand did so with two impressive new Scrambler models: a hard tail and a full-suspension bike. They’re both awesome moped-style e-bikes and handle great, but the full-suspension version definitely kicks things up a notch and allows for even wilder riding.

But since they share the same features and performance outside the rear suspension, either would make an awesome addition to your e-bike fleet. Basically, it’s a powerful yet street-legal e-bike that is designed for real-world riding, thanks in no small part to its designer, Austin Homes, being a seasoned competitive bike racer across multiple disciplines and bringing that keen eye to the Scrambler’s development.

By the time you surpass the $2,000 mark, you’re starting to get into serious e-bikes with serious performance or build quality.

If you’re spending this much, you’ll want to ensure you’re buying from a reputable company with top-notch products and service.

The Ride1Up TraiRush is the first actually quite good electric mountain bike that comes in at a budget price. It gives you a real German-made Brose motor, which offers good power matched with whisper-quiet performance. You get a dropper seat post and Maxxis Minion tires, Tektro Orion quad-piston hydraulic disc brakes, and a 120mm Rockshox Judy Silver TK Air Fork.

The first time I took a ride on the Ride1Up TrailRush, I couldn’t believe just how good the bike felt, especially from a company known for its wide array of commuter and street-oriented electric bikes. But Ride1Up was founded by cyclists at heart, and so they know what it means to build a good bike, regardless of category. At $2,095, this is a steal for just great parts.

I love the $3,299 Priority Current electric bike so much that it has become one of my daily riders. It’s the one I grab when I’m doing city riding and I know I want to be pedaling.

While the original version was already a great bike, the new Current Plus adds some key improvements, such as an included throttle and a nearly 50% larger capacity battery for more range. The throttle is great in a pinch, but the bike is also just so much fun to pedal thanks to a torque sensor-based pedal assist that provides natural-feeling assistance up to a fast 28 mph top speed.

The internally geared rear hub combined with a Gates belt drive system and powerful hydraulic disc brakes make this a very nice e-bike for the price. Plus that mid-drive motor offers tons of torque (140 Nm!) for climbing hills or accelerating quickly.

I’m also a fan of the built-in lights and included fenders, not to mention the punchy hydraulic disc brakes and Goodyear tires.

Mid-drive electric bikes aren’t cheap, but the price is definitely a fair one for this e-bike.

I’m not normally a carbon fiber, drop bar, race bike kind of guy. But then again, that’s usually because those types of bikes are way out of my price range… until now.

The Ride1Up CF Racer1 brings carbon fiber road e-bikes to the common man, offering both a traditional road bike and a gravel bike edition.

At under 29 lb (13 kg), these e-bikes are some of the lightest around. I picked one up with my index finger and was shocked at how easily it left the ground.

To get there, that meant a relatively low-power motor and a smaller battery were required. But for the folks that ride these types of bikes, a big motor and heavy battery probably aren’t on their wishlist anyway. Instead, a simple extra boost, as if you’ve always got a tailwind, helps augment your own pedaling.

For just $2,295, there’s no better deal out there for a budget-friendly carbon fiber road e-bike.

This full-suspension electric moped-style e-bike is a masterclass in high performance electric bike design. Sure, it’s an obvious SUPER73 imitation, but it’s a really well done imitation.

It handles well, it’s got great rubber between you and the road, the suspension gives a comfortable ride, and it’s got plenty of extra power.

Despite the 750W label, the e-bike can be unlocked to make use of nearly 1,500W of peak power. I was able to reach a top speed of 37 mph (59.5 km/h) during my testing (not on public streets!).

But the bike ships in Class 2 mode with a 750W limit and 20 mph (32 km/h) maximum speed, meaning you’ll have to contact the company to request an unlock for the bike to be used above Class 2 speeds and power levels.

All in all, the $2,395 Ride1Up REVV1 FS is an amazing e-bike for anyone who wants a moped-style ride with full-suspension, high quality 4-piston hydraulic disc brakes and a design that rides almost like a motorcycle, yet in a bike form factor.

Check out my complete Ride1Up REVV1 FS review here.

The Tern Quick Haul is an electric cargo bike from the famed cargo e-bike company Tern. It holds the distinction of being the most affordable model in Tern’s lineup.

That says a lot, because Tern is known for its high-quality e-bikes that are built to be ridden every day for real-world errands, carpool duty, and more. These e-bikes are meant to replace cars, and they do just that for many riders. With a huge list of accessories, you can outfit a Quick Haul for just about any job.

The Tern Quick Haul features a mid-drive Bosch motor, hydraulic disc brakes, Tern’s vertical parking rack, and a relatively lightweight but high utility design that is meant to carry just about anything in your life.

We had the chance to test a pair of Quick Hauls at Eurobike in Frankfurt last year, which you can learn more about here.

Motor: Bosch Active Line Plus mid-drive motorTop speed: 20 mph (32 km/h) with pedal assist (no throttle)Range: Up to 60 miles (96 km), less when riding in highest powerBattery: 400Wh, removable/lockableWeight: 50 lb (22.8 kg)Price: $2,999

When it comes to fast, powerful electric mopeds on a budget, it’s hard to compete with the $2,399 Ariel Rider X-Class. This is the e-bike I usually recommend when someone simply wants the most power and speed for the least amount of money.

The impressive performance comes from a powerful 1,000W motor that actually puts out closer to 2,000W of peak power. It can be limited to 20 mph if you’d like, but I got it up to 36 mph in unlimited mode.

The bike rolls on 20″ moto-style wheels and comes with nice parts including hydraulic disc brakes, a bench seat, dual suspension, and a big moto-style headlight.

You can see my full review of the Ariel Rider 52V X-Class here.

The Xtracycle Hopper is something of a Goldilocks cargo bike, in my opinion. It’s not as big as a traditional long tail cargo bike, but it’s still big enough to carry multiple kids or a week’s worth of groceries.

It’s also not as pricey as the expensive $5-7k bike shop cargo e-bikes, yet it’s much higher quality than the typical direct-to-consumer cargo e-bikes.

I’ve tested it for all sorts of cargo jobs and even put my friends and family on it to take their kids to school and let me know how it works for them. Everyone has come away impressed with the ride feel, which seems much more like a traditional bike than a bulky cargo bike.

For the money, the Xtracycle Hopper offers incredible value and much higher quality construction than you’d get with entry-level brands.

Check out my full Xtracycle Hopper review here.

Above $3.5k, you’re entering some seriously dedicated e-bike territory.

These e-bikes either feature top-shelf components like drivetrain and brake parts, or pack in so much power that they couldn’t possibly be priced any lower.

Either way, you’re not in Kansas anymore. You’re heading down the e-bike rabbit hole.

Tern is renowned for building high-quality electric bikes that double down on utility features. The newly updated Tern HSD is the company’s more affordable utility e-bike, offering many features you’ll find on its higher-end GSD but at a price that more riders can stomach.

The HSD uses small 20″ wheels and includes a folding stem, making it nice and compact. But taking it one step further, Tern outfitted it with its innovative rack that lets you park the bike on its tail, meaning it takes up barely more space than a coat rack in the corner of a room.

The bike’s Bosch mid-drive and high capacity batteries provide a high-end e-bike drive, and they are matched with a variety of performance drivetrain options that you can select from, depending on how high you want to spec the bike.

Whether you’re ferrying around kids or buying a week’s worth of groceries, the HSD is a solid option.

Check out my detailed first ride experience on the Tern HSD here.

The Xtracycle Swoop ASM is one of those electric cargo bikes that makes you rethink the entire category.

Not only does it include high-quality components and fancy features like automatic shifting paired with a high-end Shimano mid-drive motor, but it also just feels like you’re riding a normal-sized bike instead of a long and awkward cargo e-bike.

I found I can even ride it without my hands on the bars because it tracks so straight and rides so stable. That’s a feat you can’t pull off with most budget cargo e-bikes since they aren’t designed with proper bicycle geometry. But Xtracycle is a true cyclist’s cargo e-bike company, and so they understand proper bicycle design.

Check out my full review of this incredibly high-end and comfortable-to-ride cargo e-bike here.

When it comes to all-wheel-drive electric bikes, the $3,299 Ariel Rider Grizzly is one of my favorites.

This incredible e-bike is really more of a small electric motorcycle. It uses two high-power motors – one in each wheel – to offer speeds in the mid-30s and acceleration that will get you there in the blink of an eye.

Full suspension and hydraulic disc brakes make this an e-bike that handles well at any speed, and the bright red paint job looks as aggressive as the e-bike feels. It even comes with footpegs pre-installed so you can carry a passenger with you.

Plus it’s got double batteries to feed those two power-hungry motors, so you’ll be able to ride this e-bike pretty darn far too, as long as you aren’t too demanding from the throttle.

Check out my full in-depth review of the epic Ariel Rider Grizzly e-bike here.

The Priority Skyline builds upon Priority Bicycles’ legacy of creating high-performance, low-maintenance e-bikes and ups the ante with more premium parts than we’ve ever seen on a Priority e-bike before.

The show stopper here is the inclusion of Pinion’s 12-speed automatic gearbox, which smartly shifts gears without the rider ever having to think about it. You can personalize your shift points to dial in the ride, and let the centrally-mounted gearbox do the rest. The carbon belt drive transfers your pedal power while a 750W rear hub motor brings the extra power riders need.

There are also high-quality hydraulic brakes, a great suspension fork, and a big battery for long range in the city. Rear rack and complete fenders come standard, as do the built-in LED lights and adjustable handle-bar stem.

See my full review of the Priority Bicycles Skyline here.

You just couldn’t stop, could you? I showed you plenty of awesome e-bikes that didn’t cost most of a paycheck. But you just haaaaaaad to keep going.

Fine, here they are. These are some of the most expensive e-bikes out there that we’ve tested and that I’d actually recommend someone buying, if you can pony up the cash.

When it comes to ultra-premium folding electric bikes, there’s only one name that comes to my mind: GoCycle. These are the most impressive folding e-bikes I’ve ever seen. And they better be – they were designed by a former McLaren engineer.

Between the carbon fiber work, single-sided wheels, high-tech tires, trippy LED dot display, innovative rear suspension, and completely enclosed drivetrain, there’s a lot of high-end stuff going on here.

The speed will vary from 15-20 mph depending on which country you buy the bike in, but the performance is only part of the equation. The quick folding and light, compact design of the e-bike is the real winner here.

Check out my full review of the GoCycle G4i+ here.

The FREY EX Pro is a high-end electric mountain bike with crazy high power. It uses the same motor as the CSC FT1000MD we featured above (the Bafang M620 mid-drive), but cranks up the current to provide closer to 1,500W of power.

It’s enough to destroy chains if you aren’t careful. We know. Several of us here at Electrek have done it.

The FREY EX Pro not only features high-end parts like a RockShox Lyric fork and Magura MT5e hydraulic brakes, but it even comes with dual batteries so you can ride farther and stay on the trails longer.

I’ve personally taken this e-bike back UP a downhill mountain bike course – that’s how powerful it is.

It may be expensive, but it saves you several thousand dollars compared to many European electric mountain bikes with similar components but a fraction of the power in the FREY.

See my full review of the FREY EX Pro here.

The Tern GSD improves upon the Tern HSD with even more capability and even nicer components. There are multiple versions that can take you up to nearly $10k, but even the lower-tier versions are not “low tier,” if you know what I mean.

Parts like automatically engaging electric locks, 10-speed Shimano Deore transmissions, Magura MT5e quad-piston hydraulic disc brakes, and more set these e-bikes apart from the rest of the pack.

If you’re getting a Tern GSD, you’re getting a fully qualified car replacement. There are many people who use these instead of buying a second car. When you look at it like that, the price suddenly seems pretty reasonable.

Check out our full review of the Tern GSD here.

LACMA Art+Film Gala: Nicole Kidman, Timothée Chalamet honor Denis Villeneuve

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

“It’s a pleasure to be here. You guys are just crazy party people,”said artist Vija Celmins, as she summed up the jovial atmosphere at the 15th annual LACMA Art+Film Gala during her casual and off-the-cuff acceptance speech as one of the two honorees that night.

The annual fundraiser is a party, and unique among the many galas that hit the town this time of year. Along with raising money for the museum’s initiative to make film more central to the museum’s programming (this year they raised a record-breaking almost $7 million), it brings out an eclectic mix of A-list talent, up-and-coming filmmakers, and giants of the art world.

Honoree, Denis Villeneuve, Honoree Vija Celmins, co-chair Eva Chow and LACMA CEO Michael Govan

Just a quick scan of the packed room: Al Pacino sitting with artist Ed Ruscha and art dealer Larry Gagosian; artist Alex Israel wrapping his arm around Orlando Bloom for a photo; model Alex Consani crouching down to chat with influencer and comedian Quenlin Blackwell; and artists Jonas Wood and Mickalene Thomas chatting in one corner while Sarah Paulson and Parker Posey catch up in another.

Anya Taylor-Joy set the mood for the evening as the first arrival on the red carpet, where talent posed in front of Chris Burden’s famed public art installation, Urban Light. There in support of Dune director Denis Villeneuve, who was honored along with Clemins, the Dune: Part Two star wore a vintage 1997 Pierre Cardin Haute Couture bridal gown with a sheer silver overlay that looked both of the past and of the future.

Villeneuve's leading man, Timothée Chalamet, skipped the carpet all together, instead slipping into the crowded tent for dinner in a seat next to Villeneuve and his wife, producer Tanya Lapointe. Chalamet arrived separately from girlfriend Kylie Jenner, who did walk the carpet in a low-back Ashi Studio gown. When Jenner arrived at her table, she was seated opposite Chalamet, reaching across to grab his hand.

Seated next to Villeneuve was Nicole Kidman (in a voluminous, ballerina-inspired Chanel dress), who flew 15 hours from Australia to give Villeneuve his award. “The truth is I've never really met Denis. I've met him on some red carpets and sort of waved at him,” she said during her remarks. “Yet somehow I feel like I know the deepest parts of him because that is what extraordinary filmmakers do. They let you in. They reveal themselves to us through their work. They invite you into their imaginations, into their dreams, into their questions and into their fears.”

Monica Barbaro, Salma Hayek and Kim Kardashian

Before Villeneuve took the stage, a video capturing his film legacy of big, bold, and personal epics like Sicario, Blade Runner 2049, Arrival, and the Dune trilogy was played, featuring a slew of A-list directors singing his praises, including Steven Spielberg, Alfonso Cuaron, Alejandro G. Iñárritu, James Cameron, and Chloé Zhao. “I've always felt that cinema is seen as a pop art—the film carries the burden of being popular in the present,” said Villeneuve in his acceptance speech. “But at the end of the day, the real challenge is to make something that can enter the test of time.”

Speaking of tests (screen tests, that is) Villeneuve got a bit playful with the end of his speech, announcing to the room that he would right here, right now, announce which actor would play James Bond. The French-Canadian director is helming the next Bond film and over the past week, reports have been flying about who might be cast. Would Villeneuve put us all out of our misery and spill the beans? “The actor who will play the next James Bond is—and I will stop right now because [producer] Amy Pascal is about to have a heart attack,” he said with a smile.

The gala, once again presented by Gucci, hosted a slew of this Oscar season’s most exciting young filmmakers, including Obsession filmmaker Curry Barker and Club Kid’s Jordan Firstman, along with La Bola Negra directors Javier Calvo and Javier Ambrossi (known as Los Javis). The latter told me they didn’t plan to party as hard as they did at the Academy Museum Gala a few weeks ago, where they went viral for singing along to Taylor Swift's performance of "Love Story." Said Ambrossi, “I’m drinking only water tonight.”

Among the other guests, Joel Edgerton chatted with Abbott Elementary’s Quinta Brunson and Daniel Scheinert, one half of the Oscar-winning directing team Daniels (Everything Everywhere All at Once). Kim Kardashian wove her way through the crowd, pausing perfectly to look over her shoulder for a trailing photographer. And several stars made a date night out of the event: Sienna Miller brought fiancé Oli Green; Julia Garner sat with musician Charlie Walker; Viola Davis enjoyed the night with husband and producing partner Julius Tennon. Paris Hilton, who introduced Rosé onstage with her classic catchphrase “that’s hot,” wore a glittering Jenny Packham gown as she cascaded through the room with her husband Carter Reum.

Eva Chow, who had co-chaired the gala for the past 15 years with Leonardo DiCaprio, announced that she’d be stepping down from her post after this year. Dubbed the "Culture Queen of Los Angeles," she is credited with bringing together Hollywood and the art world for the event, helping grow it into the must-attend party of the season. DiCaprio couldn’t make it this year because he has a cold, but his girlfriend, model Vittoria Ceretti, was there to represent him.

But with all these big Hollywood types in the room, it was Celmins who stole the show—though she didn’t mean to. In a video that ran before her acceptance speech, the artist welcomed the video crew into her home, revealing her quick-witted humor, her ornery cat (she claims to be a dog person), and her love of Bob Dylan. Celmins is best known for her boundless work inspired by nature, including ocean waves, desert floors, starry night skies. She charmed the crowd with her boundless personality as well.

LACMA had plenty to celebrate this year, too. After a 20-year-long planning and development process, the David Geffen Galleries were completed this past year. As partygoers entered the tent for dinner, the silhouette of the formidable new building stood out in the background. “Same tent. Different buildings around it,” joked LACMA CEO Michael Govan during his opening remarks.

After his speech, Villeneuve and all the guests made their way out to LACMA’s main courtyard, where Rosé would perform under the stars. I joked with him that I thought he would make some news with that tease of a Bond casting, and then wondered if he likes being the center of attention at a gala like this. He said he values the connections he makes with other filmmakers and artists at these events. “I like talking one-on-one,” he said.

Noor Alfallah, Al Pacino, Erica Pelosini, Larry Gagosian and Ed Ruscha

While Rosé performed four songs for the black-tie-clad audience, Emily in Paris star Ashley Park and In the Heights actor Anthony Ramos swayed to her ballad, “Number One Girl.” Popcorn and cotton candy were passed out to the audience, who, naturally, sang along when Rosé ended her set with her biggest hit, “APT.” Andrew Garfield led his Artificial co-star Monica Barbaro by the hand through the crowd. Though the pair didn’t pose for photos on the carpet together, the couple (who have been romantically linked since 2025) spent the evening in close contact as they chatted with friends like Salma Hayek Pinault.

Villeneuve's speech best captured the evening’s one-of-a-kind mashup of art and film when he spoke passionately about the importance of human effort in the era of AI. “I can create something with a prompt or I can go into the desert with actors, with a crew and go on a real adventure where I learn about life and become better at my craft,” he said. “More important than the result is the process. The process is how the human experience acquires its meaning. In this moment in time, it is more important than ever that human beings keep making art together.”

Odessa A'zion in a Charles & Ron + Maison Privee coat.

Tyriq Withers, Isabel May, and Homer Gere

A Deep Dive Into How Larry Ellison Built His Influence

Top Selling Electric Vehicles in the World — 32% EV Share in September!

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Plugin vehicle registrations were up 3% year over year (YoY) globally in September, ending the month at around 2.16 million units, pretty darn close to the all-time record (2.17 million) set last December. Once again, BEVs (+8% YoY) and PHEVs (-9% YoY) are experiencing opposite dynamics. Pure electrics actually set a record high of 1.54 million registrations.

This means that, while plugin vehicle YTD growth remains low (+4% YoY), that is solely due to the PHEV blues (-10% YoY). BEVs are on their way back to normal (+12%).

Share-wise, September saw BEVs end the month at 23% share, with the plugin tally increasing to 32% if we add in PHEVs. This near-record performance pulled the 2026 plugin share to higher levels. BEVs are now at 18%, while plugin hybrids are at 7%. Therefore, 2026 EV share is now at 25%.

Looking at the best selling models, there’s some news. The leader Tesla Model Y hit a new all-time record (165,000 units), thanks to a 17% YoY growth rate. That larger six-seat version is doing wonders here.

The runner-up spot went to the Tesla Model 3. Despite being down 4% YoY, it picked up enough deliveries (65,000) to surpass both the small Geely Xingyuan, also known as the E2 or EX2 in export markets, and the BYD Song.

Regarding Geely’s hatchback, the export highlights were Brazil (2,724 units), Australia (1,211), Indonesia (1,890), and Thailand (1,759).

Despite being relegated to 4th, the BYD Song was actually the fastest grower among the top 5, rising 19% YoY and reaching 54,519 registrations. This was due to an export push (the highlights being Brazil — 5,892 units, Italy — 2,134 units, and Germany — 2,028 units) and the new-generation Ultra body. Flash charging capabilities promise to recharge the Song’s sales and make it a serious candidate, not only for the silver medal in 2026, but also as strong competition to the Tesla Model Y in off-peak months.

In a near-record month, records were plenty, with six models in the top 20 reaching record highs. Besides the aforementioned Tesla Model Y, all the other five record scorers were compact models — a coincidence or an indication of where the market is going?

The five record performers of September were:

Finally, a mention goes out to the surprise appearance of the Geely Starray / Galaxy 7, in 19th, thanks to 15,761 registrations, its best score in 20 months. With the compact PHEV crossover currently having a rather discreet career in China, export markets are giving it a new life, with the strongest markets being Australia (1,254 units in September), the UK (3,100), and … Russia (900).

Outside the top 20, there are a few models that deserve a mention. The big highlight was the landing with a bang of the MG 07, a fastback that kind of reminds me of the Porsche Taycan and Xiaomi SU7, but sits one category below, looking to compete with the Tesla Model 3 and the BYD Seal 06. It is using its sleeker silhouette to distance itself from those options. And with over 14,000 units in its landing month, one can say that MG’s new baby could become top 20 material soon.

Other Chinese EVs are also ramping up, like the Chery QQ3 (13,000 units), Xpeng L03 (12,000), BYD’s new Great Tang flagship SUV (11,000), and the Leapmotor A05 (B03 in export markets — 12,000 units). With Leapmotor on a roll, having placed two models in the top 20 in September, of all these models ramping up, I am betting that the small Leapmotor will be the one that will sit higher on the table.

Year to date, the leader Tesla Model Y is really in its own league, selling twice as many units as the runner-up BYD Song. The Model Y is so ubiquitous now that when regular people imagine what an electric car is, they think of it. Tesla’s crossover is the gold standard of EVs.

In the remaining podium positions, the #2 BYD Song and #3 Geely Xinguan held their positions, following the September high tide month of the Model 3. This is good news for both Chinese EVs. It seems the Model 3’s sales erosion continues (down 7% this year) despite the current EV sales rush caused by the future 2026 EV Salesperson of the Year, DJT.

Having said that, with 16,000 units separating Tesla’s Model 3 from the silver medal, not all is set in stone. But if I had to bet, I would say that BYD’s bread and butter model will stay in 2nd, closely followed by the Geely hatchback, and then the Model 3 ending in 4th.

(Adding context to this race, the Tesla Model 3 has been on every podium since 2018, while the Geely Xingyuan still hasn’t won any medal.)

Elsewhere, there were five changes in the table, with the first being the premium arm of BYD, Fang Cheng Bao, pulling its Land Rover Defender fighter (25,000 euros cheaper — this thing could be a huge success in Europe) into the 8th position. Below it, the Leapmotor A10/B03X continued its rise, with the crossover climbing to 12th.

Below the small Leapmotor, three compact crossovers were also on the rise. The BYD Yuan Plus/Atto 3 jumped two positions, to 14th, while the Nevo Q05 was up to 16th and its premium cousin, the Deepal S05, joined the table in 19th.

Compact crossovers are hot right now, so lucky for Changan to have two of the most competitive examples in its lineup. True, BYD also has two representatives in that category (Sealion 05 and Yuan Plus), but then again, that would have been expected — the depth of options that the Shenzhen make has is so big that the difficulty is finding a hole in BYD’s lineup. (I have a suggestion: affordable sports car? Like a Tesla Cybercab, but with pedals and a steering wheel?)

One of the biggest highlights was #2 Tesla having a record month (232,000 units, +9% YoY), but in a near record month, it wasn’t the only one to post strong results.

In fact, there were eight makes posting record results and four others performing year-best results.

#3 Geely also had something to celebrate in September, with its 130,000 registrations representing its best score of the year, mostly thanks to the fact that the Geely Xingyuan had some siblings to share the burden with this time (the Starray — 16,000 units; and the EX5 — 14,000 units). With B and C segments now covered, all they need is a strong seller in the D-segment.

Leapmotor continued on its record streak, with strong sellers in the B-segment (A10 and A05), C-segment (B10), and D-segment (C10). If there is anything currently holding back the startup, it is that the company lacks the dealership scale of BYD or Geely. But give it time….

Below the top four, we have something big happening — the rise and rise of Toyota (61,332 registrations, its fourth record in a row), which allowed it to end ahead of Volkswagen. Again. So, the Japanese globetrotter finished the month as the best selling legacy make. Again. All this despite Volkswagen placing a year-best result of 58,000 units.

Here, also, the establishment is being challenged, with the longtime leader of legacy brands being surpassed by a player that many still consider a sleeping giant.

And it’s not only Toyota’s RAV4 PHEV and BZ4X contributing to the volumes, as its Chinese operations are also delivering (the BZ3X compact crossover was the company’s third model to deliver over 10,000 units in September).

Another surprise was MG’s strong result, ending the month in 7th thanks to a record 57,000 registrations. Building on the MG 4 hachback’s success, if the MG 7 becomes the roaring success that its order list (over 50,000 units) and landing month point to, then Volkswagen will have yet another brand to worry about….

The #9 Kia is also on a record streak, having ended the month in 9th with some 50,000 registrations. With a freshly developed BEV lineup, the Korean is now sowing the seeds of an early investment in dedicated BEV platforms.

Still in Legacy Land, Mercedes has a similar story to Kia. With an ambitious BEV development plan, the German make is finally pushing its sales into record territory (40,000 units), and with new models coming in (GLA EV, C-Class EV, VLE, etc.), expect the Stuttgart brand to start getting in the rearview mirror of arch rival BMW. Maybe that pushes the Bavarian into more quickly deploying the next generation of BEVs (i4, iX1, iX2, i1, i2…).

But it seems BMW is in no hurry. I mean, even Audi had a year best result in September (34,000 units). BMW? Not even that.

The remaining highlights belonged to Chinese makes, with #15 Zeekr and #18 Chery posting record highs while #16 Fang Cheng Bao delivered year-best results. The highlight was Chery. Once known for being an ICE-heavy brand, it is now benefitting from the success of its new QQ3 EV (13,000 units) and joining the EV best seller rankings.

As for the year-to-date table, there was no major news in the top four positions, which already seem to be secured through the end of the year. However, below it, things are changing.

Rising Toyota surpassed Wuling and is now 6th. The Japanese make is some 20,000 units behind #5 Volkswagen, and while that might sound like a lot, there are still three months to go. With Toyota breaking records every passing month, it seems possible to shorten the distance by some 7,000 units every month and end ahead of Volkswagen by the end of December. To be continued…

Two more Chinese brands went up: SAIC’s MG jumped two positions, to #10, while Changan’s Qiyuan/Nevo mainstream brand joined the table at #20, much thanks to the success of the Q05 crossover.

Looking at OEMs, BYD (20.2%, down 0.1%) is stable in the lead, while runner-up Geely (9.8%, down from 9.9% in August) is holding a good distance over 3rd placed Tesla (8.7%, up 0.4% from August).

#4 Volkswagen Group (7%) is stable in 4th, just like SAIC (6.2%) is in 5th.

Behind the top 5, though, things are shaking up. Rising #6 Leapmotor (4.4%, up from 4.3% in August) is safe, but behind it, #7 Hyundai–Kia (3.9%, down 0.1%) is now being closely followed by #8 Chery (3.9%, up 0.1%). With just 3,000 units separating the two, we might see the Chinese OEM surpass the Korean in Q4.

Another OEM on the rise is Toyota (3%, up 0.1%), which joined the top 10 at the expense of BMW (2.9%, down 0.1%).

Looking just at BEVs, there were about 10.8 million registrations so far this year, or 71% of total plugin sales. A year ago, BEVs represented just 66% of plugin sales, so the PHEV slowdown is quite visible.

BYD (15.8%, up 0.3%) is at the top, benefitting from the OEM’s recent shift into BEVs. It is followed by Tesla (12.2%, up 0.6%). In 3rd place, we have Geely (8.8%), which is also safe in the last place of the podium, so the medal positions should be decided by now.

Meanwhile, there was a position change below the podium, with SAIC (6.3%) surpassing Volkswagen Group (6.3%, down 0.1%), so the Shanghai OEM is the new 4th placed. But with fewer than 4,000 units separating the two, anything can still happen.

Outside the top 5, Leapmotor (4.6%, up from 4.5% in August) climbed into the 6th position, at the expense of Hyundai–Kia (4.4% share, down 0.1%).

As an Easter Halloween Egg, here are the top sellers in the pickup truck, MPV, and sports car categories:

With the Ford F-150 EV now part of the past, a new pickup king is among us, and it is part of BYD’s stable. With the following two models selling roughly half of its volume, and a China market entry said to be coming soon, expect BYD’s Shark model to rule this category in the coming years.

Having said that, a certain Toyota Hilux BEV is ramping up, having delivered around the same number of units in September as the Cybertruck and the Ranger, so expect the 2027 race for the podium to be more entertaining, especially if a certain Slate ramps up production without major hiccups.

Now, this was a surprise. Media-friendly VW ID.Buzz? Way behind, at roughly half of the current winner. Giant Chinese minivans? The best one is only 3rd, closely followed by the Voyah Dreamer. MPVs are mostly surviving extinction because of Asian markets, and it shows. The category leader is a midsize MPV, and while its main market is its domestic China, others, like Thailand and Indonesia, where it’s known as Darion, also provide significant volumes to Wuling’s MPV.

But an even bigger surprise is the 2nd placed Vinfast MPV 7 (Limo Green in ride hailing/taxi spec). The compact seven seater, which is a sort of MPV disguised as a crossover (not unlike certain NIO designs), has most of its sales coming from native Vietnam, but some export markets are also contributing to the volume, like India, Indonesia, and the Philippines.

This is a category in crisis. With no new players; the 2026 winner, the Dodge Charger, experiencing crashing sales (down 89% YoY); and the Ferrari 296 also suffering from shrinking sales, the only highlight is the Mercedes CLE, which went from 3rd last year to it current uncontested leadership position.

Still, expect this category to receive some much needed fresh metal in 2027, like the Denza Z (of course BYD had to get into the action…), the Porsche 718 twins (Boxster and Cayman), Audi’s take on the Porsche 718 (Audi TT?), and the next-generation Alpine A110.

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

Lando Norris Wins Brazilian Grand Prix from Pole in Intense Mixed Conditions

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Verstappen nearly grabbed a win from a new grid position, driving from 21st to second, but Lando Norris and McLaren made all the right decisions to keep Norris out front at the end.

A rainy weekend in Sao Paulo led to an intense Grand Prix that ultimately saw the pole sitter, Lando Norris, take the win in a race that was anything but straightforward.

In the early laps in wet conditions, generational drivers among the sport's veterans took the lead, with Verstappen moving from 21st to ninth in the first 10 laps and Hamilton from 22nd to ninth on the opening lap.

Up front on the opening lap, Kimi Antonelli and Liam Lawson around pole sitter Lando Norris.

By lap nine, Hamilton found himself battling his teammate, Charles Leclerc, after the bad blood between them following the Mexico City Grand Prix. Hamilton loses out on the spot and starts to fade, asking to come in early for slicks as the track starts to fade. Hamilton asks to switch on lap 17 after losing 13th to Fernando Alonso's Aston Martin.

“I want to come in soon for some slick tires. Can I come in now?

Hamilton was told by his engineer that they are not at the crossover yet with the track still drying in difficult tires.

Hamilton is the first to pit, along with Carlos Sainz in the Williams, on lap 15.

On lap 17, Norris, Russell, Hadjar, Lawson and Piastri pit, with Russell spinning in the pits. Russell, on pit exit, hit a wet spot and slowly lost it, turning 180 into the way of Hadjar. It took a three-point turn to right it and exit the pits, losing positions in and out. Russell was running third before the mistake and seventh after.

Under pressure from Norris, Antonelli spins from the lead of the Grand Prix on lap 20 in turn one. Antonelli radioed that something felt weird moments before. Antonelli kept the spin clean and rejoined in second place.

Antonelli starts to struggle, feels like he can’t turn left in the corners and that the car is steering itself. He loses third to Verstappen on lap 25.

Verstappen, while running second, was losing speed as his teammate Hadjar had speed behind him. Verstappen asked to pit again on lap 47 for new soft tires to try and run through the grid a second time. Verstappen came out of the pits in front of Russell.

Norris also pitted on lap 50. Everyone is sticking with soft tires, but rain is threatening for the final ten laps.

Norris also pitted on lap 50. Everyone is sticking with soft tires, but rain is threatening for the final ten laps. The rain returns on lap 59 of 71. On lap 62, overtaking was disabled as conditions became mixed.

The rain squeezes everyone back in to pit on laps 62 and 63.

Hamilton, still on the slick tires, spins on lap 63 but keeps it out of the barriers and finds himself pointed at the pit lane. Hamilton pits from seventh and loses only one spot to Leclerc after his spin.

Victoria Beaver is a nomadic sports writer who spends her time hopping between race tracks and hippie farms. She’s covered every corner of motorsports that will let her in from 410 Sprints to NASCAR to Supercross. Her daily driver is a 2010 Subaru that she refused to do the smallest amount of preventative maintenance on. Instead, she spends her free time and money building a 42-foot Skoolie to one day travel the country full time.

My Abysmal Enterprise Car Rental Experience In Omaha

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Attending to a personal matter in Omaha with little notice, I booked a flight, hotel, and car rental. The miracle of points and miles is the ability to make anything happen when you really need it to without significant expense or inconvenience and I recommend to anyone that asks me about the loyalty space to leave a reserve to of 2.5x standard rates for an emergency trip home.

The flight was unremarkable (and priced about at that range, unfortunately), the hotel was a remodeled but otherwise unremarkable Hampton Inn, well located for my visit. But my typical rental car provider, National (owned by ERAC) was unavailable at the airport. I have some status with Avis and Hertz due to credit cards and reciprocal status but wanted to try the Drive Alliance angle for Enterprise companies. However, this required a nearby city location and not an airport rental. Honestly, it wasn’t that big of a deal to grab a Lyft to the downtown location at 23rd & Harney, and because of a credit, the ride only set me back $6.97 before tip.

Upon arrival, I was informed that my preferred car type was unavailable. For my trip a few vehicle types were offered, the cheapest being a mystery vehicle, but I opted for 2-4 levels above that for a Standard car and paid more for it. Not only was I told that the car I paid more for was not available, but that they only had minivans and there were zero other choices.

“Car choices aren’t guaranteed when booking within 24 hours.” There’s a little more context due, I know that car types are never guaranteed, but typically there’s more than just one type left.

“I don’t see that on my confirmation, can you show it to me?” She couldn’t, of course, then stated that “St. Louis”, ERAC headquarters, isn’t connected to their inventory. This opens a host of other questions. I searched the website, then I tried Google, then I enlisted AI and none of those options showed me specific adjustments described by the branch attendant stated by ERAC specific to the 24-hour booking period. I reached out to the brand on October 20th, it has yet to respond.

I had a choice to make, either take what they have or move on. As I was not at the airport and couldn’t just walk to another counter, I accepted my fate and moved to the garage.

The most unsavory part about the Enterprise experience as it differs from other brands is the endless damage evaluation. Walking around with an iPad, the attendant points out a knick here, a scratch there – the perfect moment to take out the insurance or pay with a card that includes the Collision Damage Waiver just to avoid participation.

I can’t say for certain that the vehicle was used to move pounds of marijuana across state lines – I couldn’t say that at all. But I could say for certain that it smelled so deeply of weed that I doubt any amount of cleaning could be effective.

“This one smells like weed.” I said, shutting the door.

“I’ll have to take your word for it.” She replied and moved on.

Really? Would you have noticed if I said it smelled as though a den of skunks had made their home in the stow-and-go or would you have to take my word for it? I don’t want to show up to family smelling like backstage at Coachella.

We moved on to the vehicle next to it, a California tag with 51,000 miles and a quarter tank of gas. She started the walk around again, noting that the chip in the windshield could spider but I wouldn’t be responsible if the windshield needed to be replaced upon my return. Cool. How about not giving me a vehicle where I might have a spiderwebbed safety issue?

I tossed my luggage inside, and addressed her one more time.

I had to address the fuel level, as I might not make it to my first stop without the low fuel light illuminating.

“A quarter tank?” I was borderline ready to leave but knew that I didn’t have time to start this all over somewhere else.

“Those two guys in the wash bay sitting down on their phones – do they work here?”

“Yes.” She said it to me unironically without connecting the challenge of the cleanliness, or the useless fuel level and how both of those service challenges might have been solved (in the case of the skunky van, maybe not) by the staff being asked to do their jobs.

They aren’t understaffed, they are unbothered, indifferent, and at a minimum unwilling to ask them to perform their duties, or more likely, incapable of actual management. It’s a crazy position to take with Enterprise’s excellent corporate structure and the fact that they give you the tools to be your own boss.

I guess some things are just better in the movies.

In the words of our 43rd US President, “Fool me once, shame on – shame on you. Fool me – you can’t get fooled again.” It was simpler times when that sort of turn of phrase failure was the height of presidential gaffes.

I don’t care that National’s ownership is the same (ERAC), I will continue to rent from them as long as their model remains the same and continues to deliver. But from Enterprise? Never, ever again. Maybe a “neighborhood location” has different rules, but the staff at the Downtown Omaha location exhibited a total lack of interest in my experience or their own jobs. Corporate didn’t respond to my survey nor to my request for comment. And while I would agree that I’m not Cooper Anderson reaching out to them, readers in the past have asked if the brand has a comment. They did not.

I shouldn’t have to drive around in a dented safety concern reeking of weed while I get gas immediately after departing the parking lot and somehow try to perfectly return with a quarter tank or pay $7/gallon. It’s disrespectful to your clients but it appears the great corporate structure at Enterprise may have given this branch the tools to be their own boss, and they were even less interested in doing that than I am in renting from any Enterprise location anywhere.

Besides renting from them in the first place, my greatest regret was that I didn’t get to try my credit card’s Collision Damage Waiver on for size, just in case I needed it.

Join our mailing list for a daily summary of posts! We never sell your info.

Kyle is a freelance travel writer with contributions to Time, the Washington Post, MSNBC, Yahoo!, Reuters, Huffington Post, Travel Codex, PenAndPassports, Live And Lets Fly and many other media outlets. He is also co-founder of Scottandthomas.com, a travel agency that delivers "Travel Personalized." He focuses on using miles and points to provide a premium experience for his wife, daughter, and son. Email: sherpa@thetripsherpa.comEmail: sherpa@thetripsherpa.com

I have had terrible experiences with Enterprise in the past – being stranded with a disabled vehicle that could have been prevented by adequate maintenance, spurious cleaning charges, etc., I was forced to use Enterprise due to being at remote locations recently and was very pleased with their service. Sorry you were not as lucky.

I had a bad experience with Enterprise many years ago and stopped using them. A couple of years ago I booked with them when National was too expensive and had a good experience, and I have mostly booked with Enterprise ever since. All my recent experiences have been good. However, I have been booking almost exclusively at large or medium sized airports, so that could be the difference. They almost always have skip the counter and except at the largest airports they often share cars with National and Alamo anyway.

For best results, in an off airport location, try to use a car dealership. Perhaps one that has a relationship with friends or other local contacts. There you will find a few newer rentals in great condition that are not of skunk smell. These are not advertised but kept for the company. Cannot say they would honor t & c but at least you get a better choice.

You rented from Enterprise (at a non-airport) location. What could go wrong? Well, you learned what could go wrong.

From my experience, I’m not sure Enterprise is the problem. You wrote “maybe a neighborhood location has different rules” and I don’t think it’s different rules but perhaps different standards. And to be clear, this is the case for every rental car brand where I go to a neighborhood location – I used to do this often when I didn’t have a car, and I know that it is very different than the airport experience. Point by point… – Gas tank: more likely to be less than full, but that close to empty is particularly bad. In many neighborhood locations it is literally one person working the place. Still you need enough gas to drive it off the lot. – Cleanliness: Unacceptable. That sucks and reflects poorly on the brand. – Damage/Inspection: They always do the walkaround, and there’s almost always some damage they note. Many of mine had divots in the windshield or rear windshield and a couple door dings. Combined with there being one employee this process often means renting a car takes 20-30 minutes per person, which is another issue. – 24 hour rule: I don’t know of anywhere this is written but it is absolutely true at multiple brands that those booked within 24 hours are basically considered walk-ups in terms of availability. In fact there are usually printed signs when I go to the location saying as much. I rented from an Avis in a Portland suburb in the summer and multiple printed signs and a single unhappy employee greeted at least two people who had made reservations in the last day, showed up at 8am opening, and were turned away wholesale with no cars available on a random Thursday. I’m glad this story confirmed my assumption that it had to do with connection to real time inventory at corporate locations, as many neighborhood ones are franchises.

I do not disagree that this is a terrible experience, I’m just saying this is par for the course. I’ve rented at Enterprise, Hertz, and Avis neighborhood locations all with near-identical experiences, in San Diego, Portland, and San Francisco, for what it’s worth. Usually the neighborhood locations *inside* actual car dealerships (like a full-service Nissan dealer) are much better than the standalone or hotel-attached ones in the same city and have fewer of these problems, but they also tend to be open more limited hours.

Alex is right. Never use off-airport locations if you can avoid it. Most do not have good standards, and you are lucky if they have any decent car resembling the one you reserved available. If you have to, you might have slightly better luck at Hertz or Avis.
